Massive gravity applications for T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} deformations
Alexia Nix ♭ \flat , , & Evangelos Tsolakidis ♭ \flat ,

Abstract
We employ the massive gravity approach to stress-tensor deformations in a variety of scenarios, obtaining novel results and establishing new connections. Starting with perturbation theory, we show that the addition of tr ⁡ T + Λ 2 \tr T+\Lambda_{2} to T ​ T ¯ T\overline{T} can be recovered and we construct the deformed action of an interacting non-abelian spin-1 along with spin-1/2 seed model, extending previous findings. As a result, a set of algebraic properties for certain hypergeometric functions is derived, allowing us to initiate the algebraic study of special functions directly via stress-tensor deformations and massive gravity. Moreover, we sharpen the connection between the trace-flow equation and the local renormalization group in any dimension. In d > 2 d>2 , the usual initial condition for the coupling leads to a potential known as ghost-free, minimal massive gravity. Upon expansion around the reference background, we retrieve Fierz-Pauli at leading order, matching the random geometry and holographic approaches. At the same time, we demonstrate that a change of coordinates interpretation is possible for the corresponding operator, which we verify with a simple example. Finally, we study the family of ( tr ⁡ T ) n (\tr T)^{n} deformations advancing earlier work, and illustrate how the massive gravity description of non-linear electrodynamics can be incorporated in our framework.

Contents

1  Introduction

This work explores various novel gravitational aspects of the
T\overline{T}
program and its extensions. To set the stage, is it considered standard practice in QFT to discard operators with mass dimension greater than the one of the ambient spacetime under the RG flow process, as they only become important in the UV or treat the deformed theory as an EFT. Over the past decade however, considerable effort has been dedicated towards understanding the physics of deformations with such peculiar properties in a more systematic way. In more detail, most results in this research direction are derived in two dimensions and involve the well-known by now
T\overline{T}
operator
[
72
]
i.e. the determinant of the stress-tensor of a theory. The underlying motivation comes from multiple, and sometimes orthogonal viewpoints, as recent discoveries paint the picture of an emerging interdisciplinary field, which we also aim to highlight with this paper.

Historically, these deformations were considered as another tool one can use to map out the space of all possible QFT’s
[
67
]
, however at the same time interesting connections to string theory and integrability were noted
[
17
]
based on earlier results
[
16
]
. In short, a concrete classical connection between the Nambu-Goto action and the
T\overline{T}
deformation of a CFT was established, and a closer look resulted to a match for the corresponding energy spectrum (on a cylinder) as well, which was found to be precisely the one of closed strings in a constant
B
-field under certain identifications. Soon after, the discovery of the holographic interpretation of this operator
[
55
]
(see
[
42
]
for a more recent approach) revealed another avatar of this program where the deformed CFT could be thought of living on a radial slice in the bulk of AdS
3
, at depth inversely proportional to the deformation parameter
\lambda
. These results were later extended to arbitrary dimensions
[
68
,
45
]
, providing in parallel a higher-dimensional operator which could serve as the analogue of
T\overline{T}
. Around the same time, the single trace version of this deformation was proposed
[
40
,
39
]
, where interesting connections to little string theory were noted. In another intriguing formulation
[
29
,
28
]
, flat JT gravity coupled to a QFT was found to be equivalent to the
T\overline{T}
deformation of that QFT in flat spacetime, with the uplift to a curved background appearing in
[
69
,
54
]
. The gravitational dynamics in that case are governed by a particular type of ghost-free massive gravity, which was already well-studied in various dimensions, see e.g.
[
24
,
25
,
46
,
47
]
. A closely related approach considers a spin-2 Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation as the source of the deformation
[
14
]
, which can be matched to the linearized massive gravity description obtained by the leading order expansion in
\lambda
.

The aforementioned topics focus mainly on defining reformulations of
T\overline{T}
, but there are some other interesting extensions we would like to mention.
1
1

1

The list that follows is by no means exhaustive, for a pedagogical introduction or a recent review one may refer to
[
51
,
48
]
.
A nice framework for obtaining the classically deformed action was constructed in
[
11
]
and
[
37
]
. There also exists a family of non-relativistic deformations which usually involve the current of a
U(1)
symmetry
[
43
,
38
]
. In the same spirit, several connections to non-Lorentzian geometry have been pointed out
[
10
,
44
,
9
]
. Furthermore, deformed correlators have been studied, see for example
[
53
,
15
,
1
]
. Other formulations of geometrical origin include
[
20
,
21
]
, which are also valid in higher dimensions. It is worth noting that there are by now a number of generalizations of stress-tensor deformations, with the most prominent example being root-
T\overline{T}

[
32
]
, a four-dimensional version of which
[
4
,
33
]
was found to be related to a non-linear conformal extension of electrodynamics, known as ModMax theory
[
6
]
.

With this paper, we aim to further establish our previously noted connection between massive gravity theories and stress-tensor deformations. The central relation that governs the dynamics of all our findings is

\displaystyle\partialderivative{S_{\la}}{\la}=\mathcal{O}\,,

(1.1)

which will be referred as the flow equation. The parameter
\lambda
will serve as the coupling of the operator
\mathcal{O}
which implicitly depends on it, and
S_{\lambda}
is the deformed action. As briefly mentioned above, our results have an interdisciplinary character. Remarkably, employing our method allowed us to derive various non-trivial algebraic identities for certain hypergeometric functions, extending this way a collection known as Schwarz’s list
[
64
,
12
]
. On the purely gravitational side of the story, we find that our framework very naturally connects and extends elements from the holographic renormalization of AdS
d+1

[
68
,
45
,
23
,
5
]
with the very well-known ghost-free minimal massive gravity theory
[
46
,
47
]
in
d
dimensions. At the same time, we readily demonstrate the existence of dynamical coordinates as a tool for obtaining either deformed actions or saddles for the special class of operators we proposed, extending the two-dimensional formalism of
[
20
]
. We also show how the massive gravity interpretation of the Born-Infeld theory
[
34
]
fits very naturally in our description, and as a result we provide a concrete way of deforming any Weyl invariant theory in four dimensions. While these constitute our main contributions, we further test our structure in other contexts, verifying its robustness in every case.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
we review the massive gravity techniques used to analyse stress-tensor deformations in
d
dimensions and supplement the existing literature on this topic with new universal results. We continue our analysis in Section
3
with applications to two- and one-dimensional theories, extending previous findings. In parallel, using the massive gravity approach we propose an algorithm that can potentially determine whether a given special function can be expressed as a solution in radicals, and we comment on the relation between the local RG flow and trace-flow equations. In Section
4
, we study a variety of deformed theories and their corresponding gravitational dressings, gaining novel insights from both perspectives. We collect our main results in Section
5
, discuss possible future directions, and close with a few comments on deformations for pure gravity, highlighting important caveats stemming from the first versus second order formalism. Finally, in Appendix
A
we summarize useful properties of the elementary symmetric polynomials.

2  Deformations via massive gravity

[

]

(2.5)

Taking a step back, we understand that (
2.4
) essentially forces
G
into containing all the information regarding the deformation
\mathcall{O}
. In other words,
S_{G}
in (
2.1
) is a dressing that is exclusively responsible for the deformation, leaving
S_{0}
untouched. But this is precisely one of the most important properties of stress-tensor deformations, they are valid for any seed theory! At the same time, as
\lambda\rightarrow 0
we understand that one recovers only the seed action which now lives on the reference background i.e.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Big|_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}=\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,,\qquad G(0,\mathfrak{g}_{i},d,\dots,d)=0\,.

is the undeformed stress-tensor given by the variation with respect to the auxiliary background and the bar indicates which terms are evaluated on

. Another important consequence of (

) reads as follows

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bar{S}_{\lambda}}{\delta(\star)}=\frac{\delta\bar{S}_{0}}{\delta(\star)}\,,

(2.10)

, any variation of the deformed action with respect to anything

that is not a background vielbein is equal to the same variation but of the undeformed action. This essentially illustrates that the deformed and undeformed matter equations of motion are the same on
\mathchoice{\bar{e}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bar{e}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bar{e}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bar{e}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
! Furthermore, keep in mind that the first equation of (
2.6
) should naturally drop out from the solution of (
2.8
). We have to also mention that there can exist cases such that (
2.4
) holds only when (
2.8
) is true, complicating significantly our attempt to specify
G
. Interestingly, we will discover in subsection

that the theory of non-linear electrodynamics falls precisely within this category.

2.1
Initial conditions

Before we move on to the next section, let us provide more insight regarding the initial condition (
2.6
). For the simplest case --the one we will consider for the entirety of this work-- where there are no other dimensionful couplings available, the massive gravity density
8
8

8

From (
2.1
) the mass dimension of
G
is
d
.
factorizes
9
9

9

It is obvious that in the presence of another dimensionful coupling say
\mu
, there exists a function such that
\quantity[\varphi(\mu,\la)]=M^{0}
, therefore the factorization does not hold as
\varphi
can now combine freely with
y_{i}
.
as follows

\displaystyle G(\lambda,0,y_{1},\dots,y_{d})=\lambda^{d/\Delta_{\lambda}}K(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\,,

(2.11)

with
\Delta_{\lambda}
being the mass dimension of
\lambda
since
y_{i}
is dimensionless by construction. Considering the dimensional analysis above, we conclude that
\lambda
cannot be a marginal parameter. This also means that marginal operators like root-
T\overline{T}
do not have a massive gravity description on their own, they instead dress the dimensionless variables
y_{i}
in a particular way
[
71
,
3
]
. Looking back to (
2.6
), we observe that if
\Delta_{\lambda}>0
then the second equation is trivially satisfied even if we do not set
y_{i}=d
. This is problematic since in that case,
G
is not aware that the limit
\lambda\rightarrow 0
also enforces
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248 . We are left with only one option;
\Delta_{\lambda}<0
which automatically makes the deformation irrelevant. It is quite remarkable that one can extract so many properties about our theory only via dimensional analysis. Returning to the initial condition, we have to be very careful since
G
approaches infinity due to the negative
\Delta_{\lambda}
, unless
K
approaches zero as
\lambda^{-\alpha d/\Delta_{\lambda}}
, where
\alpha
is greater than one. This implies that
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
has to be an implicit function of
\lambda
, which we choose to be the solution of (
2.8
) that respects (
2.6
) as
\lambda
approaches zero. Expanding now
G
around
\lambda\rightarrow 0
we obtain the following

\displaystyle G(\lambda,0,y_{1},\dots,y_{d})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{n+d/\Delta_{\lambda}}\frac{1}{n!}\derivative[n]{K(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})}{\la}\Bigg|_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\,,

(2.12)

and since
G
should approach zero as
\lambda^{(1-\alpha)d/\Delta_{\lambda}}
we find that necessarily

\displaystyle j_{1}\cdots j_{n}\partial_{y_{j_{1}}\dots y_{j_{n}}}K(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\Big|_{y_{i}\rightarrow d}=0\,,\quad n\leq-(1+\alpha d/\Delta_{\lambda})\,,\quad\alpha d/\Delta_{\lambda}\in\mathds{Z}_{<0}\,,

(2.13)

where we used that for
n\geq 1
, we have
\mathchoice{f^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{f^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{f^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{f^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}\differential\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\mak One can imagine that instead of matching the exact powers of
\lambda
when the negative integer condition for

\lambda^{(1-\alpha)d/\Delta_{\lambda}}

We are now ready to put everything together and completely determine how the initial conditions should behave based on our previous analysis. If we truly want to work with a universal gravitational dressing, its initial conditions cannot depend on the choice of
S_{0}
, which affects
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

\displaystyle\alpha=2\,,\qquad\Delta_{\lambda}=-d\,.

(2.16)

Using these, we finally arrive at

\displaystyle K(d,\dots,d)=0\,,\qquad j\partial_{y_{j}}K(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\Big|_{y_{i}\rightarrow d}=0\,,

(2.17)

where the first equation is essentially (
2.6

to initiate at linear order in
\lambda
, therefore if one would be interested in an expansion that begins at
\lambda^{n}

, the initial conditions generalize to

\displaystyle K(d,\dots,d)=0\,,\qquad j_{1}\cdots j_{n}\partial_{y_{j_{1}}\dots y_{j_{n}}}K(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\Big|_{y_{i}\rightarrow d}=0\,,\qquad n\geq 1\,,

(2.18)

when (
2.16
) is valid.

These results will be sufficient for us to proceed to the next sections, where we will confirm their validity and provide new insights about stress-tensor deformations in various dimensions.

3  Implications in two dimensions

We begin by specifying the generic operator that will be used for the entirety of this section

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\quantity{b_{0}\tensor{T}{^\mu_\nu}\tensor{T}{^\nu_\mu}+b_{1}(\tensor{T}{^\mu_\mu})^{2}+\frac{b_{2}}{\la}\tensor{T}{^\mu_\mu}+\frac{b_{3}}{\la^{2}}}\,,

(3.1)

with
(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
being dimensionless coefficients due to (
2.16
) and considering (
2.11
) the corresponding ansatz for the massive gravity will be

\displaystyle S_{G}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential[2]{x}f\,\{c_{0}+c_{1}y_{1}+c_{2}y_{1}^{2}+c_{3}y_{2}\}\,.

(3.2)

Using only (
2.4
) and without specifying any initial conditions yet, we reach the following solution

11

11

Here
b_{0}\neq 0
. The case where

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}b_{1}=-b_{0}\,,\qquad b_{3}=-\frac{b_{2}(1+b_{2})}{2b_{0}}\,,\\
c_{0}=\frac{1+b_{2}}{2b_{0}}\,,\qquad c_{2}=b_{0}c_{1}^{2}\,,\qquad c_{3}=-c_{2}\,,\end{gathered}

where we notice that
(b_{0},b_{2})

\lambda

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt\quantity{-\det T+\frac{b_{2}}{\la}\tr T-\frac{b_{2}(1+b_{2})}{\la^{2}}}\,,\quad S_{G}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential[2]{x}f\,\quantity{1+b_{2}-y_{1}+\det Y_{1}}\,.

We must note that the derived operator above with arbitrary
b_{2}
has a string theoretic interpretation once we derive its spectrum
[
71
]
, by considering the deformation of a CFT on a cylinder of circumference
R
. Briefly, one finds

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{n}=-\frac{R\quantity(1+2b_{2})}{2\lambda}\pm\sqrt{\quantity(\frac{2\pi p_{n}}{R})^{2}+\quantity(
\frac{R}{2\la})^{2}+\frac{\mathcal{E}_{n}}{\lambda}}\,,

(3.7)

where
p_{n}\in\mathds{Z}
and
\mathcal{E}_{n}/R
corresponds to the undeformed energy levels. Proceeding with the following identifications

\displaystyle\lambda\leftrightarrow\dfrac{\alpha^{\prime}\pi}{w}\,,\quad b_{2}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{\mathchoice{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}{B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{0}{1}}}}-1}{2}\,,\quad p_{n}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{N_{\text{L}}-N_{\text{R}}}{w}\,,\quad\mathcal{E}_{n}\leftrightarrow\dfrac{2\pi\quantity(N_\text{L}+N_{\text{R}}+k_{\bot}^{2})}{w}\,,

(3.8)

the spectrum above matches the one of a closed string in a
B
-field
[
22
]
when
p_{\bot}^{2}=2k_{\bot}^{2}/\alpha^{\prime}
. The role of
b_{2}
is now clear, it corresponds to a non-trivial value for the
B
-field. With this in mind, we move on to the next subsection where we will verify independently via perturbation theory that the operator (
3.5
) necessarily assumes this form.

3.1
Perturbation theory for
T\overline{T}+\tr T+\Lambda_{2}

Here, we will show that one does not need to write down the specific ansatz of (
2.1
) for the deformed action, i.e. using the massive gravity paradigm, in order to independently reproduce the operator (
3.5
). One can instead write down the most generic deformed action of a two-dimensional theory in terms of the Lagrangian density

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\hskip 1.42271pt{\mathcal{L}}_{\lambda}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{L}}_{\lambda}=\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\lambda^{n}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\,,

(3.9)

where we wrote the Lagrangian density as a Laurent expansion
12
12

12

Please note that the operator (
3.1
) implies that terms proportional to
\lambda^{-1}
will also be part of the expansion.
in the deformation parameter
\lambda
and the only assumption we make is that the Lagrangian density of the undeformed theory is given by
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
.
In this language the stress-tensor is given by

\displaystyle\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}=\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\lambda^{n}\mathchoice{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\,,\qquad\mathchoice{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{(T_{n})^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][ (3.10)

and in matrix notation its determinant reads

\displaystyle\det T=\sum_{n,m=-1}^{\infty}\lambda^{n+m}\det T_{n,m}\,,\qquad\det T_{n,m}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\tr T_{n}\tr T_{m}-\tr(T_{n}T_{m}))\,.

(3.11)

Moreover, the relation we obtain from the flow equation (
1.1
) when the deforming operator is (
3.1
) with
b_{0}=-b_{1}=1/2
and
b_{2},b_{3}
undetermined is

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}n\lambda^{n-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=-\sum_{n,m=-1}^{\infty}\lambda^{n+m}\det T_{n,m}+b_{2}\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\lambda^{n-1}\tr T_{n}+b_{3}\lambda^{-2}\,.

(3.12)

We continue by solving this equation order by order in powers of
\lambda
. At
\lambda^{-2}
we arrive at a PDE for
{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
due to the relation of
T_{-1}
. However, its solution requires further initial/boundary conditions which we do not have as we only know about
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
. For this reason, the only acceptable solution for
{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
is the one that does not implicitly depend on the background
\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. Then, the PDE reduces to a simple algebraic equation which can be written as

\displaystyle b_{3}={\mathcal{L}}_{-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{-1}-2b_{2}-1)\,.

(3.13)

On the other hand, at
\lambda^{-1}
one can easily show that
b_{2}={\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
for the same reasons and thus
b_{3}=-b_{2}(1+b_{2})
, matching the operator in (
3.5
) on the nose! Having determined the negative powers in
\lambda
, the remaining equations (
3.12
) at positive powers in
\lambda
reduce to the known perturbative solution for
T\overline{T}

[
17
]

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\det T_{m,n-m-1}\,,\qquad n\geq 1\,,

(3.14)

which is completely specified in terms of the undeformed Lagrangian density
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
.

3.2
Hypergeometric is geometric

The goal of this subsection is to investigate the algebraic properties of special functions via stress-tensor deformations of a two-dimensional QFT. To do so, we will apply the massive gravity method to two specific seed actions. The first one is given by a non-linear sigma model in a potential, while the second is an interacting Yang-Mills-like theory with Dirac spinors. We then show how the resulting deformed action can be recast as an infinite series of hypergeometric functions and provide an infinite extension to Schwarz’s list
[
64
]
.

Let us start our analysis with the seed action of the non-linear sigma model that describes the interaction of bosons and fermions with a non-trivial potential, that is

\displaystyle S_{0}=-\int\differential[2]{x}e\quantity{\frac{1}{2}\tensor{g}{^\mu^\nu}\tensor{\Phi}{_\mu_\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\tensor{e}{^{\mu}_{a}}\tensor{\Psi}{^{a}_{\mu}}+V}\,,

(3.15)

where the bosonic and fermionic kinetic terms are respectively contained in
13
13

13

Here we define the symmetrization of the indices as
\mathchoice{A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85803pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32607pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.09004pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{(\mu}{\nu)}}}}{A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85803pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32607pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.09004pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{(\mu}{\nu)}}}}{A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85803pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32607pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.09004pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{(\mu}{\nu)}}}}{A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.98734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85803pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47003pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1343pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32607pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.09004pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{(\mu}{\nu)}}}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2!}(\mathchoice{A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{ and the antisymmetrized covariant derivative as
A\vecev{\nabla}_{\mu}B\coloneqq A\vec{\nabla}_{\mu}B-A\cev{\nabla}_{\mu}B

(3.19)

Let us now restrict to the case of a single boson without fermions. We choose to study the deformed action one obtains by considering the positive branch of the solution (
3.18
) as this is the one with a smooth
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit. After some simplifications we get

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}=\int\differential[2]{x}f{\mathcal{L}}_{\lambda}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{1}{2\lambda(1-V\lambda)}\quantity(1+ \sqrt{1+2\la(1-V\la)X})\Bigg\}\,,

(3.20)

where we introduced the shorthand notation
X\coloneqq\mathchoice{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\Phi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{\Phi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{\Phi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{\Phi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
.

We proceed by obtaining the deformed Lagrangian density via a perturbative expansion in
\lambda
.
However, it should not matter whether one studies the expansion around
\lambda\rightarrow 0
or instead expands for small
V
. The reason this latter expansion can be performed without loss of generality is because the potential always appears in the deformed action as a product with the deformation parameter
\lambda
, i.e. in the form of
V\lambda
. By considering the aforementioned approach, we find the following answer
14
14

14

This expression is equivalent to the one found in
[
17
]
.
for the deformed Lagrangian density

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\lambda}=-\frac{V}{1-V\lambda}-\frac{X}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n-1)!!(\lambda^{2}VX)^{n}{}_{2}\widetilde{F}_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\frac{1}{2}+n,1+n}{2+n};-2\lambda X\biggr]\,,

(3.21)

where we defined the tilded hypergeometric function as the regularized hypergeometric function via

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widetilde{F}_{q}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{p}}{b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{q}};c\biggr]\coloneqq\frac{1}{\Gamma(b_{1})\Gamma(b_{2})\cdots\Gamma(b_{q})}{}_{p}F_{q}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{p}}{b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{q}};c\biggr]\,.

(3.22)

By appropriately combining the above results we find that the infinite sum of hypergeometric functions resums to the following expression

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n-1)!!(yw)^{n}{}_{2}\widetilde{F}_{1}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\frac{1}{2}+n,1+n}{2+n};-2w\biggr]=\frac{\sqrt{1+2w(1-y)}-1}{w(1-y)}\,,

(3.23)

where we introduced the variables
y\coloneqq V\lambda
and
w\coloneqq X\lambda
. Expanding now the right hand side for small
y
, one can obtain the algebraic form for this particular
{}_{2}\widetilde{F}_{1}
family by comparing both sides of (
3.23
) for every
n
. In this case, one retrieves already known results.

In our above analysis we restricted to the case of a single boson. However, it would be interesting to extend our results to the case of
N
bosons and examine whether this could yield further insight into the closed form expression in terms of radicals, of particular infinite sums of special functions. We leave this for future studies.

Let us now switch gears and continue our analysis by studying a different seed action, namely

\displaystyle S_{0}=-\int\differential[2]{x}e\quantity{\frac{1}{4}\text{tr}(\tensor{F}{_\mu_\nu}\tensor{g}{^\mu^\rho}\tensor{F}{_\rho_\lambda}\tensor{g}{^\la^\nu})+\frac{1}{2}\tensor{e}{^{\mu}_{a}}\tensor{\Psi}{^{a}_{\mu}}+V}\,,

(3.24)

where the trace is taken over the suppressed gauge group indices. Here, the covariant derivative
\mathchoice{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}
of (
3.17
) now also contains a connection term coming from the gauge field, i.e.
\mathchoice{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}\rightarrow\mathchoice{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{D^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}
.
Finally, assuming that the field strength is the usual antisymmetric two-tensor, the subsequent analysis holds for both abelian or non-abelian gauge fields. Considering the factorization of the deformed action (
2.1
), this argument is also valid for any other stress-tensor deformation.

With the above in mind, we may now once again solve the equations of motion coming from (
3.5
) with
b_{2}=0
, together with the above seed action to extract a solution for the auxiliary zweibein; that is

\displaystyle\mathchoice{(e_{s,\pm})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(e_{s,\pm})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(e_{s,\pm})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(e_{s,\pm})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\frac{H_{s,\pm}}{1-\lambda V}\mathchoice{\hat{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hat{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hat{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hat{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,,\qquad\mathchoice{\hat{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hat{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox
