pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,,

(3.25)

with

\displaystyle H_{s,\pm}=\frac{1}{4}\Bigg(1+s\sqrt{\pm\frac{2}{\sqrt{x}}+3-x}\pm\sqrt{x}\Bigg)\,,\qquad s=\pm 1\,,

(3.26)

where we defined

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}x\coloneqq\frac{3P}{2(P+\sqrt{P^{2}-P^{3}})^{1/3}}+\frac{3}{2}(P+\sqrt{P^{2}-P^{3}})^{1/3}+1\,,\\
P\coloneqq\frac{64F^{2}\lambda}{27(f^{-1}\hat{f})^{2}}(1-V\lambda)^{3}\,,\qquad F^{2}\coloneqq\text{tr}(\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}\mathchoice{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\rho}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\rho}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\rho}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\mu}{\rho}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\rho}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\rho}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\rho}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\rho}{\lambda}}}}\mathchoice{\gamma^{{{\lambda}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\lambda}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\lambda}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\gamma^{{{\lambda}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})\,.\end{gathered}

Moreover, the requirement of the auxiliary metric to be positive definite implies that
H\in\mathds{R}
. This is true for
H_{s,+}
and yields
0<x\leq 4
,
-\infty<P\leq 1
, while the two remaining solutions
H_{s,-}
are purely imaginary or zero. These ranges imply that
F^{2}
must be bounded by a critical value, that is

\displaystyle\frac{64F^{2}\lambda(1-V\lambda)^{3}}{27(f^{-1}\hat{f})^{2}}\leq 1\,.

(3.29)

Notably, in the
V\rightarrow 0
limit and for the case where the fermions are turned off, the upper bound of (
3.29
) agrees with the critical electric field that was found in
[
12
]
.
Inserting the above solution into the deformed action (
2.1
) gives

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}=\int\differential[2]{x}f\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{F^{2}(1-V\lambda)^{2}}{4f^{-1}\hat{f}H_{s,\pm}^{2}}+\frac{f^{-1}\hat{f}H_{s,\pm}(H_{s,\pm}-2)}{\lambda(1-V\lambda)}\Bigg\}\,.

(3.30)

Clearly, when we turn off the deformation parameter in (
3.25
) we must recover the initial condition given by (
2.6
). This is achieved when
H\lvert_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}=1
, which is only true for the
H_{1,+}
solution and hence is the one we should analyse. In what follows, for notational brevity we denote
H_{1,+}
with
\mathcal{H}
.

Similarly as before, we can continue our analysis by turning off the fermions. Certainly, upon inserting the appropriate solution of (
3.26
), this action becomes highly non-trivial as it is given in terms of (powers of) cubic roots. In addition, this answer is not very intuitive. In an effort to better understand this solution we utilize the perturbative expansion outlined in the previous subsection. For our purposes, however, this is not enough and one needs to perform an additional expansion around
V\rightarrow 0
. As explained above, this can be done without any loss of generality, since once again the action depends on the potential only through the product
V\lambda
. This analysis allows us to resum the full perturbative expansion in
\lambda
to a hypergeometric function. In turn, the perturbative expansion in the potential is then given by an infinite sum of hypergeometric functions.

More concretely, we find that the deformed Lagrangian of (
3.30
) without fermions can be written as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=-\frac{V}{1-V\lambda}-\pi F^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{3^{n-5/2}(-V\lambda)^{n}}{\Gamma(1+n)}{}_{5}\widetilde{F}_{4}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\frac{1}{2},\,\,\frac{3}{4},\,\,1,\,\,1,\,\,\frac{5}{4}}{2,\frac{3-n}{3},\frac{4-n}{3},\frac{5-n}{3}};\tfrac{64}{27}F^{2}\lambda\biggr]\,,

(3.31)

where the regularized hypergeometric function is related to the usual hypergeometric function through (
3.22
). Notice that in the limit of
V\rightarrow 0
we recover the deformed action that was obtained in
[
12
]
.
By comparing (
3.31
) to the closed form expression of (
3.30
) with no fermions, we find that the infinite sum of (regularized) hypergeometric functions can be written in terms of radicals, viz.

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{3^{n-5/2}(-y)^{n}}{\Gamma(1+n)}{}_{5}\widetilde{F}_{4}\biggl[\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{\frac{1}{2},\,\,\frac{3}{4},\,\,1,\,\,1,\,\,\frac{5}{4}}{2,\frac{3-n}{3},\frac{4-n}{3},\frac{5-n}{3}};\tfrac{64}{27}z\biggr]=\frac{(1-y)^{2}}{4\pi\mathcal{H}^{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{H}-1)^{2}}{\pi z(1-y)}\,,

(3.32)

where we defined
y\coloneqq V\lambda
and
z\coloneqq F^{2}\lambda
, to clarify that this results in a Taylor expansion around
y=0
on the left hand side of the equation.

Remarkably, combining this expression, with the previous result (
3.23
) implies that stress-tensor deformations can in general be used as a tool for deriving closed form expressions of infinite sums of special functions when using the massive gravity approach. From this perspective: hypergeometric functions are geometric.

Equation (
3.32
) can provide yet another infinite extension of Schwarz’s list as one can Taylor expand in
y
the right hand side of each equation and compare both sides order by order. Then we can directly extract the algebraic form of the corresponding hypergeometric function. It is important to stress once more that the massive gravity approach is the reason as to why these special functions can be expressed in terms of these algebraic expressions.

Before we proceed to the next subsection a few comments are in order. First, throughout our latter analysis for the
T\overline{T}
deformation of (
3.24
), we considered the case without fermions but with a non-trivial potential. Although this choice is not very physical, it generated a new closed form expression for an infinite sum of hypergeometric functions through (
3.32
). Hence, we argue that even the study of seed actions that (perhaps) lack physical significance, could lead to fresh insights regarding the study of such infinite sums. Nonetheless, we aim to include fermions in a subsequent analysis. Second, it would be interesting to combine the two previous seed actions into one action functional, such as

\displaystyle S_{0}=-\int\differential[2]{x}e\quantity{\frac{1}{4}\text{tr}(\tensor{F}{_\mu_\nu}\tensor{g}{^\mu^\rho}\tensor{F}{_\rho_\lambda}\tensor{g}{^\la^\nu})+\frac{1}{2}\tensor{g}{^\mu^\nu}\tensor{\Phi}{_\mu_\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\tensor{e}{^{\mu}_{a}}\tensor{\Psi}{^{a}_{\mu}}+V}\,,

(3.33)

and examine whether performing the above analysis would generate an algebraic solution for other hypergeometric functions. Looking at (
3.33
) and restricting to the case of a single boson with no fermions yields equations of motion for the zweibein that assume the form of a sixth degree polynomial. This means that a solution in radicals does not exist. Therefore, the resulting infinite sum, which may again contain special functions, will not have an algebraic description. This fact shows that this approach can be thought of as both a go or no-go theorem!

The algorithm we followed to express certain hypergeometric functions and their infinite sums in terms of algebraic functions can be summarized as follows. First, we write down a seed action and combine this with the massive gravity action (
3.5
). From there we can extract the equations of motion for the auxiliary zweibein. If the resulting equations are a polynomial of degree less than five, then the zweibein admits a solution in radicals. Otherwise, this will not be the case. In the former, it then proves useful to perform a perturbative analysis of the corresponding flow equation, we expect that this allows one to express certain special functions algebraically.

On the other hand, starting from a special function, say
\Sigma(u)
, it is interesting to construct a reverse algorithm. This will allow one to express
\Sigma(u)
in terms of an algebraic function. Essentially, this boils down to finding the correct scalar quantity
u
to identify with the appropriate combination of (background) fields. In our previous examples this scalar was given by
w,z
. More generally, however, this scalar can be of the following form

\displaystyle u\coloneqq\lambda{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=\lambda\,(\text{background})\cdot(\text{fields})=\lambda\,\mathfrak{f}\cdot\mathfrak{u}\,,

(3.34)

where we suppressed the index structure of
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{u}
, each of which can include appropriate combinations of the vielbein, spin connection, gauge field, bosonic fields, fermionic fields and possible derivatives thereof.
15
15

15

Recall that in the massive gravity approach
\mathfrak{f}
only contained appropriate combinations of the vielbeine.
In this language, the deformed action and operator read

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}[\bar{\mathfrak{e}},\mathfrak{f},\lambda,\mathfrak{u}]=\int\differential[d]{x}f\,\Sigma(u)\,,\qquad\mathcal{O}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\,F(\mathfrak{J})\,,\qquad\mathfrak{J}\coloneqq\frac{1}{f}\frac{\delta S_{\lambda}}{\delta\mathfrak{f}}\,,

(3.35)

where
\mathfrak{e}
is the auxiliary background field and
\bar{\mathfrak{e}}
denotes its on-shell value, we come back to this momentarily.
Before proceeding, we highlight that one must find an appropriate
F(\mathfrak{J})
, such that (
1.1
) holds, i.e.
\partial_{\lambda}\Sigma(u)=F(\mathfrak{J})
which, in principle, is not an easy task.

Assuming that this can be done, we introduce the following factorization of the deformed action

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}[\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f},\lambda,\mathfrak{u}]=S_{G}[\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f},\lambda]+S_{0}[\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{u}]=\int\differential[d]{x}f\,G(\lambda,\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f})+\int\differential[d]{x}e\,{\mathcal{L}}_{0}(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{u})\,,

(3.36)

and find a solution for
\partial_{\lambda}G(\lambda,\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f})=F(\mathfrak{J})
, that satisfies the appropriate initial conditions when
\lambda\rightarrow 0
, in analogy with (
2.17
). From here it follows that one must integrate out the auxiliary field, as in (
2.7
). As mentioned earlier, if the resulting equations that one recovers for
\mathfrak{e}
, upon solving the equations of motion, are polynomials of degree less than five, one can obtain a solution for
\bar{\mathfrak{e}}=\bar{\mathfrak{e}}(\lambda,\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{u})
in radicals. Evaluating the deformed action on-shell will in turn yield a Lagrangian density that can be written as an algebraic function. Following these steps could then lead to the algebraic formulation of a given special function
\Sigma(u)
. Furthermore, our examples indicate that the introduction of an arbitrary potential in the seed action will allow one to obtain such expressions for infinite sums of special functions, when these take the form of a Taylor expansion around small V.

3.3
The local renormalization group and the trace-flow equation

The trace-flow equation, relates the expectation value of the
T\overline{T}
operator to the expectation value of the trace of the deformed stress-tensor and it was initially noted in
[
17
]
at the classical level and in
[
55
]
quantum mechanically. For this subsection, we will explain how this result is essentially a special case of a known local RG flow equation
[
27
,
65
,
57
,
58
,
59
,
50
,
60
,
61
]
(see
[
66
]
for a nice review) which will in turn allow us to compute the corresponding beta function up to all orders.

First, let us assume that our theory contains some couplings along with their scaling dimensions
(\lambda_{i},\Delta_{\lambda_{i}})
, some fields
(\Phi_{i},\Delta_{\Phi_{i}})
and the background vielbein
(\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},1)
. The next step is to temporarily uplift the couplings to sources
16
16

16

Under the RG flow, this will generate derivative terms that will contribute to the conformal anomaly, however in the end we will consider the limit
\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}(x)\rightarrow\lambda_{i}
which will make them disappear. This effect is analogous to the one we get when uplifting from a flat background to a curved one for a CFT; the price we pay is the conformal anomaly which would otherwise vanish in the flat spacetime limit.
i.e.
\lambda_{i}\rightarrow\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}(x)
allowing us to write down the following set of Weyl transformations (no sum)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(x)\rightarrow e^{\sigma(x)}\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(x)\,,\qquad\Phi_{i}(x)\rightarrow e^{-\Delta_{\Phi_{i}}\sigma(x)}\Phi_{i}(x)\,,\qquad\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}(x)\rightarrow e^{-\Delta_{\lambda_{i}}\sigma(x)}\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}(x)\,.

(3.37)

The variation of the deformed action under (
3.37
) reads (yes sum)

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}S_{\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\quantity{\frac{1}{f}\frac{\de S_{\varlambda_{i}}}{\de\tensor{f}{_{\mu}^{a}}} \tensor{f}{_{\mu}^{a}}-\frac{\Delta_{\Phi_{j}}}{f}\frac{\de S_{\varlambda_{i}}}{\de\Phi_{j}}\Phi_{j}-\frac{\Delta_{\la_{j}}}{f}\frac{\de S_{\varlambda_{i}}}{\de\varlambda_{j}}\varlambda_{j}}\sigma\,,

(3.38)

therefore from (
1.1
) and for a theory that is symmetric
17
17

17

This almost always requires the addition of curvature terms
[
8
]
.
under (
3.37
) one finds

\displaystyle\tr T=\Delta_{\lambda_{i}}\lambda_{i}\mathcall{O}_{i}+\text{curvature/eom terms}\,,

(3.39)

where
\mathcall{O}_{i}
are the integrand operators. The above is a classical equation, essentially showing that when the scaling dimension or couplings are absent, the theory is classically conformal on-shell
18
18

18

That is if
\Delta_{\Phi_{i}}\neq 0
.
for a flat background
[
52
]
. We must stress that this equation is a result of the already deformed theory. For example, consider the deformation of a CFT only via one operator with coupling
\lambda
. Combining (
1.1
) and (
3.39
) one may be tempted to write an equation that looks like
\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda\partial_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\,\tr T
and then attempt to use it as the definition for the deformation. This will however lead to trivial results for
S_{\lambda}
. Nevertheless, irrespectively of the deformation, this equation is always a good consistency check for the deformed theory.

In order to derive the quantum version of (
3.39
), we first observe that the Weyl rescaling of the background (
3.37
) takes us to larger distances (we pick
\sigma>0
without any loss of generality) therefore the RG flow we consider is from the UV to the IR. In more detail, if our bare theory is defined at
\Lambda_{0}(x)
, the RG flow will take us to
\Lambda(x)=e^{-\sigma(x)}\Lambda_{0}(x)
and as a consequence
(\Phi_{i},\text{\char 21\relax}_{i})\rightarrow(\Phi^{\text{R}}_{i},\text{\char 21\relax}^{\text{R}}_{i})
i.e. the resulting effective action is now described in terms of the renormalized fields and sources. Moreover, the partition function is unaffected by this change leading to
\mathcal{Z}_{\Lambda}[\text{\char 21\relax}^{\text{R}}_{i}]=\mathcal{Z}_{\Lambda_{0}}[\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}]
from which one can obtain a version of the Callan-Symanzik equation. We can now study the variation of the effective action under an infinitesimal change in scale which can be expressed entirely in terms of
\sigma
or equivalently by an infinitesimal change of the background vielbein. Under this transformation the renormalized fields will pick up an anomalous dimension
\widetilde{\text{\char 13\relax}}_{\Phi_{i}}(x)
and the corresponding effect for the sources is given by the beta functions
\widetilde{\beta}_{\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}}(x)
, where tilde indicates the local dependence. Gathering everything we have that

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}S^{\text{eff}}_{\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}}=\int\differential[d]{x}f\quantity{-\frac{1}{f}\frac{\de S^{\text{eff}}_{\varlambda_{i}}}{\de\tensor{f}{_{\mu}^{a}}} \tensor{f}{_{\mu}^{a}}-\frac{\widetilde{\vargamma}_{\Phi_{j}}}{f}\frac{\de S^{\text{eff}}_{\varlambda_{i}}}{\de\Phi^{\text{R}}_{j}}\Phi^{\text{R}}_{j}+\frac{\widetilde{\beta}_{\varlambda_{j}}}{f}\frac{\de S^{\text{eff}}_{\varlambda_{i}}}{\de\varlambda^{\text{R}}_{j}}}\sigma\,,

(3.40)

with
\widetilde{\text{\char 13\relax}}_{\Phi_{i}}\coloneqq-\partial\log\Phi^{\text{R}}_{i}/\partial\sigma
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{\text{\char 21\relax}_{i}}\coloneqq\partial\text{\char 21\relax}^{\text{R}}_{i}/\partial\sigma
. From there, we can finally write down the local RG flow equation

\displaystyle\tr T=\beta_{\lambda_{i}}\mathcall{O}_{i}+\text{anomalies}+\text{curvature/contact terms}\,,

(3.41)

which can now be treated as an operator equality.
19
19

19

Please note that in both (
3.39
) and (
3.41
), terms proportional to total derivatives can appear, however they will not affect our final result.
In that light, we now turn off all couplings but one
(\lambda)
, we pick a flat background, fix the dimension to be
d=2
and the choice for an operator reads

\displaystyle\mathcall{O}=-2b_{0}\det T+\frac{b_{2}}{\lambda}\tr T+\frac{b_{3}}{\lambda^{2}}\,.

(3.42)

Under the aforementioned criteria, the seed theory is most likely a CFT, which we will pick to live on a Euclidean cylinder of circumference
R
with local coordinates
(\tau,x)\sim(\tau,x+R)
and
\tau\coloneqq it
. Similarly to (
3.7
), the complete, non-perturbative quantum effect of this deformation to the undeformed spectrum is given by

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{n}=-\dfrac{R\quantity(1+2b_{2})}{2\lambda^{\prime}}\pm\sqrt{\quantity(\dfrac{2\pi p_{n}}{R})^{2}+\quantity(\dfrac{R\qty(1+2b_{2})}{2\la'})^{2}+\dfrac{2b_{0}\quantity(b_{3}R^{2}+\la'\mathcal{E}_{n})}{{\lambda^{\prime}}^{2}}}\,,

(3.43)

with
\lambda^{\prime}\coloneqq 2b_{0}\lambda
. Now, together with

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\tensor{T}{_\ta_\ta}}{n}=-\dfrac{E_{n}}{R}\,,\qquad\expectationvalue{\tensor{T}{_\ta_x}}{n}=-\dfrac{2\pi ip_{n}}{R^{2}}\,,\qquad\expectationvalue{\tensor{T}{_x_x}}{n}=-\partialderivative{E_{n}}{R}\,,

(3.44)

we can evaluate the expectation value on either side of (
3.41
) on the basis
\ket{n}
and then solve for the beta function of
\lambda
. Reassuringly, for both energy branches one finds a rather simple result

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}=\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda\,,

(3.45)

verifying that
T\overline{T}
is indeed a protected operator in flat space
[
55
]
. An equivalent way of reaching the same conclusion is to notice that from (
3.8
),
\lambda
can be identified with
\alpha^{\prime}
which does not run under the RG flow hence the beta function would only contain the classical piece. It would be very interesting to apply these techniques to the models considered in
[
63
]
and carefully compare the resulting beta functions.

The main take-home message from this subsection is the subtle difference between (
3.39
) and (
3.41
). The first is a classical equivalence derived by considering the generalized Weyl symmetry of the action, whereas the second is an operator equation coming from the local renormalization group procedure. A direct consequence of this discussion is that an operator satisfying (
3.41
) and (
3.45
) is either automatically protected or, a large
N
expansion scheme is implicitly assumed. With that in mind we can now have a clear interpretation of our previous results
[
71
]
which also covers the holographic cases
[
68
,
45
]
. In short, the unique
20
20

20

In a sense that it corresponds to the only polynomial operator built out of finite powers of the stress-tensor that has a ghost-free massive gravity dual; more on this in subsection
4.1
.
trace-flow equation in any dimension takes the following form (in matrix notation)

\displaystyle\tr\bar{T}=\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda\Bigg\{\text{$\overline{\hbox{higher order terms}}$}+c_{1}\quantity(\tr\bar{T}^{2}-\dfrac{1}{d-1}(\tr\bar{T})^{2})+\dfrac{c_{2}}{\lambda}\tr\bar{T}+\dfrac{c_{3}}{\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\}+\dfrac{\bar{e}}{f}\tr\bar{T}_{0}\,,

(3.46)

where
(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})
are dimensionless constants and the last term essentially satisfies (
3.39
) for the couplings and the background of the undeformed action i.e.
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
and
\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. This equality was derived following a strictly classical procedure which can now be understood as a manifestation of the generalized Weyl symmetry (
3.37
). Then, the holographic approximation further assumes large
N
which automatically kills off the higher order terms. Reversing the tape, we propose (by direct comparison with (
3.41
)) that the full-blown operator equality would be given by the relation above with
\Delta_{\lambda_{i}}\lambda_{i}\rightarrow\beta_{\lambda_{i}}
as we do not generally expect the couplings to not run in a dimension greater than two.

As a final comment, we notice that for the case of
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}
–where
b_{2}=0
and
b_{3}
is arbitrary in (
3.42
)– the geometric description à la massive gravity requires the inclusion of another irrelevant coupling
\lambda_{0}
with
\Delta_{\lambda_{0}}=-2

[
70
]
, which would automatically generate a corresponding beta function and an operator, further complicating the trace-flow equation. To be more precise, this does not mean that such an equality does not exist for
T\overline{T}+\Lambda_{2}
(in fact, this is what we showed in this subsection), however if one insists on its gravitational description, extra terms will inevitably appear in the trace-flow equation, at least classically. This fact also explains why
b_{3}
is given by (
3
) under the assumption that no other couplings are present in our theory; this is the only choice allowing a massive gravity description with a trace-flow equation written entirely out of the deformed and undeformed stress-tensor.

3.4
Initial conditions for dimensional reduction

For this subsection we will verify that the gravitational description of the operator coming from the dimensional reduction of (
3.42
), matches precisely the one predicted in
[
41
]
only if the initial conditions (
2.17
) are met. Interestingly, the resulting gravitational mass term will also be well-defined at the level of the partition function.

Following the standard procedure
[
41
]
, the resulting operator takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=\int\differential{x}f\,\dfrac{T^{2}+b_{3}/{\lambda}^{2}}{1+2b_{2}-2T\lambda}\,,

(3.47)

where we fixed
b_{0}=1/2
. Then, from (
2.4
) we obtain the following solution
21
21

21

We again assume that there are no other dimensionful couplings.

\displaystyle S_{G}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential{x}f\,\quantity{\frac{1}{2}+b_{2}+\frac{c_{0}}{y_{1}}+\frac{(1+2b_{2})^{2}+4b_{3}}{16c_{0}}y_{1}}\,.

(3.48)

The first check is to see whether an equation like (
3.46
) holds for the above. A direct calculation yields

\displaystyle\bar{T}=\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda\dfrac{\bar{T}^{2}+b_{3}/{\lambda}^{2}}{1+2b_{2}-2\bar{T}\lambda}+\dfrac{\bar{e}}{f}\bar{T}_{0}\,,\qquad\Delta_{\lambda}=-1\,.

(3.49)

Applying the initial conditions (
2.17
) we find that
b_{3}=0
and
b_{2}=-(1+4c_{0})/2
, allowing us to rewrite (
3.47
) and (
3.48
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}=-\int\differential{x}f\,\dfrac{T^{2}}{4c_{0}+2T\lambda}\,,\qquad S_{G}=\frac{c_{0}}{\lambda}\int\differential{x}e\,(e^{-1}f-1)^{2}\,.

(3.50)

We can now restrict to a flat background and restore diffeomorphism invariance simply by
f=\partial_{x}\mathcal{Y}\eqqcolon\dot{\mathcal{Y}}
, where
\mathcal{Y}
is now the diffeomorphism Stückelberg field. The last step is to carefully compare with
[
41
]
achieving a perfect match for
c_{0}=1/8
, which we understand to be just a rescaling of the deformation parameter in complete analogy with
b_{0}
in the two-dimensional case. Finally, the deformation at the level of the partition function reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\int\frac{\mathcall{D}e\mathcall{D}\mathcal{Y}}{\text{vol}(\text{diff})}\exp{\frac{i}{8\la}\int\dd{x}e\,(e^{-1}\dot{\mathcal{Y}}-1)^{2}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}[e]\,,

(3.51)

where its evaluation in Euclidean signature yields consistent results. The deformed energy levels simply read

\displaystyle E_{n}=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-8\lambda\mathcal{E}_{n}}}{4\lambda}\,,\qquad E_{n}\Big|_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\,,

(3.52)

therefore with the help of (
3.41
) and (
3.49
) we find that the following equation holds

\displaystyle E_{n}=-\beta^{(n)}_{\lambda}\frac{2E_{n}^{2}}{1+4\lambda E_{n}}+\mathcal{E}_{n}\,,\qquad\beta^{(n)}_{\lambda}=\lambda(\sqrt{1-8\lambda\mathcal{E}_{n}}-2)\,,

(3.53)

where the expansion around
\lambda\rightarrow 0
gives the correct classical contribution to the beta function (assuming that this identification is valid).
Taking a step back, we notice that the described procedure of Section
2
yields a well-behaved partition function in both one and two dimensions.

We can now repeat the same arguments for the
\mathcall{O}=-T^{2}
deformation in
d=1
, obtaining this way a gravitational dressing that one can stick into a path integral
[
41
]
. In this case, (
2.4
) reduces to an ODE and applying (
2.17
) returns a unique solution. The effect on the partition function in the diffeomorphism invariant flat space limit is now simply given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\int\frac{\mathcall{D}e\mathcall{D}\mathcal{Y}}{\text{vol}(\text{diff})}\exp{\frac{i}{\la}\int\dd{x}e\,\qty(\sqrt{e^{-1}\dot{\mathcal{Y}}}-1)^{2}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}[e]\,,

(3.54)

where we notice that a highly non-linear root structure emerges compared to (
3.51
). It would be very interesting to study the properties of the kernel above, and check whether if one could obtain the anticipating effect on the deformed energies. We leave this for future work. For completeness, the energy levels read

\displaystyle E_{n}=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{n}}{\lambda\mathcal{E}_{n}-1}\,,\qquad E_{n}\Big|_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\,.

(3.55)

At the classical level, we can obtain the correct trace-flow equation using (
3.54
) i.e.

\displaystyle\bar{T}=-\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda\bar{T}^{2}+\dfrac{\bar{e}}{f}\bar{T}_{0}\,,\qquad\Delta_{\lambda}=-1\,,

(3.56)

which now suggests the following analogue for the beta functions

\displaystyle E_{n}=-\beta^{(n)}_{\lambda}E_{n}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{n}\,,\qquad\beta^{(n)}_{\lambda}=\Delta_{\lambda}\lambda+\mathcal{E}_{n}\lambda^{2}\,.

(3.57)

Another classical calculation would be to consider the effect of this deformation on say, a sigma model of bosons in a potential. Once again, this would not be a very well motivated calculation from the physics aspect, however for the case of a single boson special functions identical to the ones we retrieve in subsection
3.2
make their appearance
[
41
]
. This indicates that one can use the algorithm we developed previously to study their properties and provide other types of extensions to our results.

Overall, for an operator
\mathcall{O}(\lambda,T)
the governing equation for the gravitational dressing would be given by the solutions of (
2.4
) which reduces to an ODE when there are no other couplings present. Together with (
2.17
), the complete set of equations read

\displaystyle K(y_{1})=-\lambda^{2}\mathcal{O}(\lambda,[K(y_{1})-y_{1}K(y_{1})^{\prime}]/\lambda)\,,\qquad K(1)=0\,,\qquad K(y_{1})^{\prime}\Big|_{y_{1}\rightarrow 1}=0\,,

(3.58)

where
K(y_{1})^{\prime}\coloneqq\differential K(y_{1})/\differential y_{1}
and the units of
\mathcal{O}
are restricted to be
M^{2}
. Building on this lower-dimensional intuition, we will repeat the same process in the next section but for higher dimensions.

4  Applications in higher dimensions

In this section, we will attempt to provide a direct explanation of the extra terms that appear in the higher-dimensional generalization of
T\overline{T}
that we propose, by understanding the physical content of the gravitational dual. In turn, this will allow us to make solid and highly non-trivial predictions for the deformed field theory. We will then apply our methodology to other higher-dimensional guesses and show that they all fit very nicely into our formalism.

4.1
Minimal massive gravity

We will begin by recalling the structure of the unique, order
d
polynomial expression (in terms of
y_{i}
) for the gravitational dressing that corresponds to a polynomial operator of order
d
entirely constructed out of the deformed stress-tensor. It is important to mention that the result was achieved after an exhaustive procedure, where the most general polynomial ansatz of order
d
for the massive gravity dressing and operator was used. This automatically means that we have already covered all possible cases of dRGT mass terms
[
24
,
25
]
that could have a stress-tensor deformation interpretation of the type we just mentioned. The resulting dressing is of the following form (in matrix notation)

\displaystyle S_{G}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential[d]{x}f\,\quantity{c_{0}+c_{1}y_{1}+c_{2}\det Y_{1}}\,,

(4.1)

where
(c_{0},c_{1},c_{2})
are arbitrary dimensionless constants, and we will provide the corresponding operator momentarily. Applying the initial conditions (
2.17
), we retrieve
c_{0}=c_{1}(1-d)
and
c_{2}=-c_{1}
where
c_{1}
is once again an overall rescaling of
\lambda
. Looking back to (
3.5
), we pick
c_{1}=-1
reaching

\displaystyle S_{G}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential[d]{x}f\,\quantity{d-1-y_{1}+\det Y_{1}}\,.

(4.2)

The first thing to notice is that for
d=1
this action vanishes identically, therefore one needs to consider the deformations and gravitational mass terms of subsection
3.4
. Furthermore, since there are no other free parameters, the corresponding integrand operators are completely fixed, and their expressions are given by

\displaystyle\mathcall{O}_{d}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\quantity[1+\la\tr\mathcal{T}-\det(\mathds{1}_{d}+\la\mathcal{T})]\,,\qquad\mathcal{T}\coloneqq T-\frac{\tr T}{d-1}\mathds{1}_{d}\,.

(4.3)

We can rewrite the above in terms of power-traces of the stress-tensor however, the formulæ get significantly more complicated as we travel to higher dimensions, therefore we will only provide the first three

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcall{O}_{2}=&\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\tr T^{2}-\frac{1}{1}(\tr T)^{2}]=T\overline{T}\,,\\
\mathcall{O}_{3}=&\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\tr T^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\tr T)^{2}]-\frac{1}{3}\lambda\quantity[\tr T^{3}-\frac{3}{4}\tr T^{2}\tr T+\frac{1}{8}(\tr T)^{3}]\,,\\
\mathcall{O}_{4}=&\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\tr T^{2}-\frac{1}{3}(\tr T)^{2}]-\frac{1}{3}\lambda\quantity[\tr T^{3}-\frac{1}{2}\tr T^{2}\tr T+\frac{1}{18}(\tr T)^{3}]+\\
&+\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}\quantity[\tr T^{4}-\frac{8}{9}\tr T^{3}\tr T-\frac{1}{2}(\tr T^{2})^{2}+\frac{5}{9}\tr T^{2}(\tr T)^{2}-\frac{11}{162}(\tr T)^{4}]\,.\end{aligned}

(4.4)

One can then easily check that the these operators satisfy (
3.46
) with
\Delta_{\lambda}=-d
on-shell for the auxiliary background and notice that the higher order contributions could be treated as
\lambda
corrections to the usual
T^{2}
operator of
[
68
,
45
]
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcall{O}_{d}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\tr T^{2}-\frac{1}{d-1}(\tr T)^{2}]+\text{finite $\lambda$ corrections}\,.

(4.5)

Since the leading piece is valid at large
N
it is very natural to postulate that our result includes (possibly all) finite
N
corrections.
22
22

22

Assuming that large
N
implies small
\lambda
and vice-versa.
However, we must mention that the stress-tensor in the expressions above is always the deformed one, meaning that there is an implicit dependence on
\lambda
. This is very important because in order to properly perform the power counting one needs to expand everything and rearrange the furniture appropriately. The situation now becomes very complicated since the leading piece will contribute a term at order
\lambda
, complementing the zeroth order contribution from the first
\lambda
correction. Similarly, at
\lambda^{2}
one gets a piece from the second order expansion of
T^{2}
plus a term from the first order expansion of
T^{3}
but also the zeroth order part of
T^{4}
(for
d\geq 4
). From this discussion it becomes evident that an actual
\lambda
expansion looks like

\displaystyle\mathcall{O}_{d}=\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\tr T_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{d-1}(\tr T_{0})^{2}]+\text{infinite $\lambda$ corrections}\,,

(4.6)

where
T_{0}
is the undeformed stress-tensor which is independent of
\lambda
. It is now very easy to confuse (
4.5
) with (
4.6
), since they both have the same structure and especially when it comes to comparisons with the holographic approaches
[
68
,
45
]
. The Brown-York stress-tensor there seems to be the deformed one, but the Hubbard-Stratonovich interpretation
[
14
,
45
]
seems to prefer the undeformed stress-tensor. These are conflicting arguments that will require further investigation.

The field theory analysis we presented is not very illuminating and still does not explain why these corrections are there in the first place. For this reason, we will shift our attention to (
4.2
) where we have the advantage of working with a relatively simple expression, which also holds for any
d
. Understanding the dynamics there would then reduce to “just” translating them in a field theory language.

This mass term seems unfamiliar, however we can rewrite it in terms of the corresponding metrics instead by finally utilizing the gauge freedom we have. Our choice will be the symmetric vielbein condition
[
49
,
26
]
which simply reads

\displaystyle\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\eta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\eta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\ (4.7)

and as a result (
4.2
) can now be written as

\displaystyle S_{G}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential[d]{x}\sqrt{-\gamma}\,\quantity{d-1-\tr\sqrt{\g^{-1}g}+\det\sqrt{\g^{-1}g}}\,.

(4.8)

Remarkably, this dressing is well-known among the massive gravity community and was initially derived in
[
46
,
47
]
for
d=4
. It goes by the name “ghost-free minimal massive gravity” and it belongs to the family of dRGT mass terms
[
24
,
25
]
. Very similar constructions also appear in three dimensions, see for example
[
7
]
. The mass parameter is related to the deformation coupling via
m^{2}=\#\kappa^{2}/\lambda
where
\kappa^{2}
is the usual gravitational coupling constant and
\#
some normalization factor. Let us now rewrite the above recalling that the auxiliary background contains all the implicit
\lambda
dependence. We can conveniently parametrize this metric as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}=\mathchoice{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c 2pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{g^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}\Bigg|_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\,,

(4.9)

where we essentially factor out the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
behaviour, therefore (
4.8
) can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{G}&=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\differential[d]{x}\sqrt{-\gamma}\,\sum_{\kappa=2}^{d}\text{det}_{\kappa}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\quantity[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{i}(2\g^{-1}h)^{i}](2\gamma^{-1}h)^{i}\\

(4.10)

where
\text{det}_{\kappa}
is given by (

). The first thing to notice is that in a
\lambda
expansion, the leading piece is precisely the Fierz-Pauli mass term
[
46
,
47
]
. Plugging the above in (

) and expanding the seed action in the same way one obtains

\displaystyle (4.11)

where crucially the undeformed stress-tensor is not an implicit function of
\lambda
. This is precisely the construction considered in
[
45
]
where the metric
\mathchoice{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
is understood as the Hubbard-Stratonovich field that needs to be integrated out in a similar way to ours. However, notice that the stress-tensor we consider here is the undeformed one. The solution to the equations of motion is simply

\displaystyle\mathchoice{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{h^{{{(1)}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}=\lambda\quantity(\tensor{{T_{0}}}{_\mu_\nu}-\frac{\tr T_{0}}{d-1}\tensor{\g}{_\m_\n})\,,

(4.12)

therefore the on-shell action is now

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}=S_{0}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int\differential[d]{x}\sqrt{-\gamma}\,\quantity[\tr T_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{d-1}(\tr T_{0})^{2}]+\dots\,,

(4.13)

which is very clearly the first order correction to the undeformed action under the deformation (
4.4
). One can then keep generating more corrections by integrating out the higher-order auxiliary backgrounds reaching, eventually, the full effect of the deformation i.e. (
4.3
), covering at the same time the holographically derived
T^{2}
part. The conclusion we draw from this analysis is that the random geometries framework seems to be valid only at leading order in the
\lambda
expansion if one considers just the contribution from the Fierz-Pauli mass term.

Combining all the information of this subsection, we observe that the
T^{2}
operator is related to the linearized Fierz-Pauli dressing (and its derivative corrections) whereas the full
T^{d}
deformation comes from its non-linear completion, which enforces the absence of fictitious gravitational degrees of freedom
[
24
,
25
,
46
,
47
]
. Therefore, we finally understand that the higher-order corrections appearing in the deforming operator are necessary since they ensure that spin-2 ghosts are not present in the deformed theory! This can only be seen when we include kinetic terms for the auxiliary background in the mix, which automatically complicates the process of integrating it out. It would be very interesting to further investigate the properties of this gravitational mass term in a more applied setting, so the next subsection will be devoted to that.

4.2
Dynamical coordinates

Coming back to (
4.2
), we can now combine it with the undeformed action
S_{0}
, take a flat space limit and restore diffeomorphism invariance in an exact analogy to the previous section. It is then very natural to postulate that the deformed partition function will be

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\int\frac{\mathcall{D}e\mathcall{D}\mathcal{Y}}{\text{vol}(\text{diff})}\exp{\frac{i}{\la}\int\dd[d]{x}\det(\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{Y}^{a})\qty(d-1-\pdv{x^{\mu}}{\mathcal{Y}^{a}}\tensor{e}{_\mu^a}+\frac{e}{\det(\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{Y}^{a})})}\mathcal{Z}_{0}[e]\,,

In two dimensions, the left hand side then gives precisely the integrability condition, however this is not true any more in every other higher-dimensional case. The reason behind this behaviour is very simple and goes beyond the specific deformation we consider so let us briefly explain. There are only two conditions that enter this argument, the integrability constraint
\partial_{[\mu}\partial_{\nu]}\mathcal{Y}^{a}=0
and the conservation of the undeformed stress-tensor
\partial_{\mu}\mathchoice{{T_{0}}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{T_{0}}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{T_{0}}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{T_{0}}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=0

d

d

d(d-1)/2

(4.20)

It is crucial to mention that in all three equalities, the deformed and undeformed Lagrangian as well as the undeformed stress-tensor are expressed in the
x^{\mu}
coordinate system thus, in order to obtain the actual deformed theory one needs to also re-express
everything in terms of
\mathcal{Y}^{a}
via (
4.20
). This is not a trivial task, and it is understandable that all the complexity of this process is hidden in this last step, especially when one wants to check the validity of these expressions. To circumvent this, we will consider the simplest example possible, which avoids entirely this issue and leave more complicated cases for future work. Therefore, we pick a theory consisting of just a potential without any kinetic terms, in other words

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=-V\,,\qquad T_{0}=-V\mathds{1}_{d}\,.

(4.21)

It is then very easy to deduce that the deformed theory is exactly (
4.19
) for this choice. If all these statements are true, the deforming operator must be given by (
4.3
) and a quick check confirms that everything is indeed consistent.

Taking a step back, we observe that our gravitational formalism and deformation leads to a non-trivial generalization of earlier two-dimensional results
[
20
]
and provides a concrete way of generating field-dependent coordinate transformations that yield actions deformed by a stress-tensor operator. One can notice that the source of these findings is yet again the mass term (
4.2
) and its properties. Before we move to the next subsection where we explore yet another aspect of this approach, we should highlight the importance of (
2.10
). This equation clearly states that the field saddle points of the deformed and undeformed actions match, which is a strong hint for the existence of a map between the two. But it should be clear from our discussion that this is exactly the content of (
4.20
), therefore we conclude that given a seed theory and the solution to its equations of motion on a flat background, one can directly obtain the deformed solution via (
4.20
).

4.3
Non-linear electrodynamics

In this subsection we will take a closer look into the massive gravity description of the Born-Infeld action in four dimensions, generalizing it to the case of a curved background and as a by-product, we provide a concrete way of deforming any Weyl invariant seed theory in four dimensions. We begin from the curved version of the dressing
